`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **A2UI + AG-UI are complementary layers, not competitors:** - **A2UI** (Google, v0.8): Declarative JSON format describing *what* the UI looks like — a flat list of components with properties and a data model. The agent outputs JSON, not code. The client maintains a "catalog" of trusted components and renders them natively. - **AG-UI** (CopilotKit): Event-based streaming protocol for *how* the agent communicates with the UI — messages, tool calls, state updates. It's the transport layer. - Together: Agent Spec defines what runs, AG-UI carries the interaction, A2UI defines what the user sees. **Key A2UI design:** Security-first (no executable code, just data), LLM-friendly (flat list easy to generate), framework-agnostic (same JSON renders in React, Angular, Flutter, etc.). `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Now let me fetch the actual A2UI spec and AG-UI event types:

The security posture on the rendering side is strong — no `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` or `eval` paths in the new A2UI code. Several issues require attention before this merges: a wildcard CORS header on a production streaming endpoint, a missing default `type` field on `TextMessageEvent` that will raise at runtime, an over-broad click trigger guard in the renderer, and a `useCallback`/`useEffect` dependency cycle in `useUniverseStream` that risks infinite reconnect loops. --- ### Findings #### CRITICAL - **`/home/mike-anderson/dev/cohezion/.worktrees/genesis-tdd-a2ui/src/cohezion/api/routes/agui.py:174`** — `"Access-Control-Allow-Origin": "*"` is set directly on the `/api/agui/stream` `StreamingResponse`. This bypasses any origin whitelist that the FastAPI CORS middleware enforces, because per-response headers override middleware. If this endpoint is reachable from a browser, any origin can read the SSE stream. Remove the manual CORS header and rely solely on the global CORS middleware, or scope it to a known-safe origin. - **`/home/mike-anderson/dev/cohezion/.worktrees/genesis-tdd-a2ui/src/cohezion/api/agui_events.py:97`** — `TextMessageEvent` inherits from `AGUIEvent` but provides no default value for the inherited `type: AGUIEventType` field. The base class `AGUIEvent` defines `type` without a default. Python dataclass inheritance requires that fields with defaults appear after fields without defaults. Since `TextMessageEvent` adds only defaulted fields (`message_id`, `role`, `delta`) while `type` is non-defaulted and comes first, instantiating `TextMessageEvent(type=AGUIEventType.TEXT_MESSAGE_START, ...)` works only because the caller passes `type` explicitly — but if any caller omits it (or a future subclass is added), …

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **OI-MAS Confidence Scoring Architecture**: The confidence computation combines three signals: 1. **Model quality** (30%): Static quality tier from MODEL_QUALITY 2. **Historical success rate** (40%): Dynamic, learned from execution tracking 3. **Complexity-model alignment** (30%): Penalizes mismatches (small model + complex task = 0.5) Plus a **degradation factor** (0.8x) when the system is in active degradation cooldown, which acts as a proxy for the TopologicalRouter's PIVOT regime. This closes the gap between the four independent routers — they now share confidence signals through the degradation feedback mechanism established in Session 82. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Now let me check on the research agents and move to Phase 3 (TurboQuant). > AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **TurboQuant Verification**: PolarQuant exploits the angular structure inherent in manifold vectors — by quantizing in polar coordinates instead of Cartesian, the geometric relationships (which are angular for SU(2) spinors and Bloch sphere states) are preserved even at 4 bits. QJL's 32x compression for cosine similarity is remarkable — a 256D float32 vector (1024 bytes) becomes a 32-byte bit string, yet cosine similarity is preserved to ~0.05 accuracy. This maps directly to SemanticCache L2 where we only need similarity comparisons, not exact vector reconstruction. **HIHO Connection (L223)**: QJL's sign-only encoding is literally HIHO at the bit level — each projection produces half positive, half negative signs. The equilibrium point is 50% matching bits. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` Now Phase 4 — LatentMAS agent communication. Let me implement the FLUME vector channel:

<task-notification> <task-id>ad00d973ad7381f1c</task-id> <tool-use-id>toolu_012MsN3d5wUvdMpXgo2TM2CP</tool-use-id> <output-file>/tmp/claude-1000/-home-mike-anderson-dev-cohezion/b64da2bf-bc43-4e98-92b9-9ef978ee0301/tasks/ad00d973ad7381f1c.output</output-file> <status>completed</status> <summary>Agent "Code review of Session 84 changes" completed</summary> <result>I now have enough information to write a thorough report. --- ## Code Review: 10 files from Session 84 ### Summary The session introduces solid new capabilities — curriculum reward shaping, bootstrap evaluation, latent agent communication, and unified routing — with consistently good non-blocking patterns. However, there are two type-safety defects that would cause runtime crashes, one logic error in router statistics, and several standards violations including missing tests for three new files and missing `__all__` declarations in new public modules. --- ### Findings #### CRITICAL - **`src/cohezion/compound/executor.py:471`** — `vault_experiment_path=None` is passed to `ExecutionResult`, but the field is declared as `str = ""` (line 66). Passing `None` where `str` is expected violates the type contract and will fail mypy strict mode. Any caller that does `result.vault_experiment_path.startswith(...)` will raise `AttributeError` at runtime. The field should use `str | None = None` or the call site should pass `""`. - **`src/cohezion/eval/universe_evaluator.py:164`** — The `logger.info` format string contains `%.1%%` which is an invalid `%`-style format specifier. The `%%` escape in a logging format string with positional args will be interpreted literally as two percent signs in some Python versions but is logically inconsistent: `convergence_rate * 100` is being formatted and then `%%` appended. This will raise `TypeError: not all arguments converted during string formatting` at runtime because `logger.info` uses `%`-style, not f-string, formatting. The string should read `"conv_rate=%.1f%%"` (drop the extra `%`). --- #### HIGH - **`src/cohezion/swarm/cost_aware_router.py:984-985`** — `get_statistics()` queries `query_count_per_model` using `"qwen3-coder:30b"` and `"deepseek-r1:70b"` (lines 984–985), but the canonical model keys used throughout the file are `"qwen3-coder:32b"` and `"deepseek-r1:8b"` (see `MODEL_COSTS`, `select_model`). `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **SAC underperforms random at 20K steps** — reward 1.38 vs 6.72, 0% convergence vs 20%. This is expected behavior for SAC with limited timesteps: SAC's off-policy replay buffer needs more samples to build a useful Q-function (typically 50-100K for continuous control). At 20K steps, SAC is still in its exploration phase with high entropy. **Key diagnostic**: Random outperforms SAC because (1) SAC's entropy bonus encourages exploration that fights the Lagrangian attractor, and (2) the replay buffer hasn't accumulated enough successful trajectories to learn the reward structure. The greedy baseline (50% convergence, 11.46 reward) shows the physics attractor's natural guidance — any policy that moves toward 0.5 on brane dimensions succeeds. **Next step**: Train SAC for 100K steps, or reduce entropy coefficient (`ent_coef=0.05` instead of default `auto`). `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **Fascinating result — PPO dense slightly underperforms random (-1.79) but BEATS greedy (+3.73).** This inverts the usual hierarchy. The dense reward's simpler signal actually helps PPO find a policy that's more reward-efficient than the greedy baseline, but random exploration in the dense regime happens to accumulate more reward than PPO's learned policy. This completes the **2×2 algorithm-reward matrix**: | | Curriculum | Dense | |---|---|---| | **PPO 100K** | **14.23** (vs random +7.51) | 38.95 (vs random -1.79, vs greedy **+3.73**) | | **SAC 100K** | 10.91 (vs random +8.59) | **40.77** (vs random +3.40, vs greedy -1.20) | **Key pattern**: PPO works better with curriculum (structured on-policy learning benefits from staged objectives). SAC works better with dense (simpler Q-function target). Neither algorithm dominates — the reward structure must match the algorithm's learning dynamics. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Now I have enough context. Let me build the GEMM v2 tiled kernel. The key insight is that the v1 naive kernel has each thread compute one output element — that's terrible for GPU utilization. V2 needs shared memory tiling with cooperative thread loading. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **GEMM Tiling for AMD MI355X (gfx950):** 1. The naive kernel (v1) is memory-bound: each thread independently reads A and B from global memory, causing massive redundant loads. Shared memory tiling lets a thread block cooperatively load tiles of A and B, then each thread reuses the shared data across multiple K iterations. 2. MI355X has 304 CUs with 8 XCDs (Accelerated Compute Dies). Optimal tile sizes need to fill all XCDs — BLOCK_M=128, BLOCK_N=128 gives 1 tile per ~16K output elements, which maps well to the CU count for typical GEMM shapes. 3. The MXFP4 format packs 2 values per byte with E8M0 scales (one scale per 32 elements). Tiling along K in chunks of 32 aligns perfectly with the scale group boundary. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

This is a clean, battle-tested load_inline variant. The key difference from our v2: it uses the "lifted scale" pattern (accumulate unscaled dot products per block, then multiply by scales once) but is still a naive 1-thread-per-element approach. Our v2 should be faster due to shared memory tiling. However, this autoresearch version has a critical difference — it uses the **shuffled** B weights and scales (B_shuffle, B_scale_sh) instead of re-quantizing B from scratch. This is important: B is pre-quantized and pre-shuffled by `generate_input()`. Let me check if our v2 kernel is correctly using the pre-processed B data. Looking at our v2 `custom_kernel`, we re-quantize B from scratch: ```python B_q_raw, B_scale_e8m0 = dynamic_mxfp4_quant(B.contiguous()) ``` This is wasteful — B is already quantized in the input tuple. The autoresearch version uses `B_shuffle.view(torch.uint8)` and `B_scale_sh.view(torch.uint8)` directly. However, their kernel receives shuffled data, while our v2 kernel expects un-shuffled layout. The fundamental issue: **shuffled** data is optimized for aiter's internal tile coalescing pattern, not for our custom kernel's sequential byte access. Our v2 should use **un-shuffled** B data. Let me fix this:

> AGENT

Right — our v2 re-quantizes B from bf16, which adds ~26µs of overhead. We should use the pre-quantized `B_q` instead. But the issue is that `B_q` from `generate_input` is in `dtypes.fp4x2` format (not plain uint8). Let me fix the kernel to use the pre-quantized data directly:

> AGENT

Critical finding from the skill: **`torch.*` and `aiter.*` calls DO work on the correct stream** because they go through PyTorch's C++ dispatch layer. The problem is only with direct HIP API calls (ctypes, hipModuleLaunchKernel). But `load_inline` is different from ctypes — it compiles a PyTorch extension with pybind11 that calls `<<<grid, block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>`. This SHOULD work because it uses PyTorch's stream API, not raw HIP. Let me check what exactly happened with our load_inline submission:

> AGENT

Confirmed — load_inline still triggers the stream error. The key question: is it the `load_inline` compilation itself (which spawns a subprocess), or the kernel launch? Let me create a minimal test that only uses load_inline for a trivial passthrough:

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **Critical Discovery: `load_inline` compilation ITSELF triggers the stream error.** The minimal test doesn't even call the compiled module — `custom_kernel()` only uses aiter. But the `load_inline()` call at module import time spawns `hipcc` compilation which creates a HIP context/stream as a side effect. The runner's stream scanner detects this during the import phase, before `custom_kernel()` is ever called. **This means load_inline compilation must be deferred or avoided entirely.** The runner scans for stream violations at module import time, not just during kernel execution. `─────────────────────────────────────────────────` This changes everything. Let me test if we can defer the compilation to inside `custom_kernel()`:

> AGENT

Still fails even with deferred compilation. The runner's stream scanner is detecting `load_inline` compilation itself — likely because `hipcc` subprocess initializes a HIP context. Let me try one more thing — what if we don't even import `load_inline` and the submission just does the source scan check?

> AGENT

The pure aiter submission WORKS — it compiles the JIT modules and loads the pre-compiled `.co` kernels successfully. This confirms: **`load_inline` is the problem, not HIP kernel launches in general.** The aiter JIT system uses `hipModuleLoad` (loading pre-compiled kernels) which works fine. `load_inline` uses `hipcc` subprocess compilation which creates a conflicting HIP context. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────` **The Stream Mystery Solved (L264):** 1. **aiter JIT works**: It loads pre-compiled `.co` files via `hipModuleLoad` + `hipModuleGetFunction`. These route through the correct stream because they're loaded into the existing HIP context. 2. **load_inline is blocked**: It spawns `hipcc` (a subprocess) which initializes its OWN HIP context during compilation. This new context creates a new default stream. The runner's scanner detects this as "work on another stream." 3. **The path forward**: Instead of compiling HIP C++ at runtime via load_inline, we need to either (a) use aiter's existing pre-compiled `.co` kernel files, or (b) find a way to compile without creating a new HIP context, or (c) use `torch.cuda._compile_kernel()` (the experimental HIPRTC API from the research that may avoid context creation). `─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

> AGENT

This is a major pivot. The "load_inline is the only path" assumption from the entire Session 88B plan was WRONG. Let me check what the actual Rank 1 approach is — they're at 4.4µs, they must be using something that works on the runner:

The search found something important: the **`torch.cuda._compile_kernel()` API** using HIPRTC (not hipcc subprocess) compiles in 0.01s and might avoid the context creation issue. This example uses `load_inline` without explicit stream handling — BUT this is for CUDA (NVIDIA), not HIP (AMD). The AMD MI355X runners may have different stream isolation rules.
